Here’s an example.</span></p><p><b><span>Problem 431.</span></b></p><blockquote><p><span>Math problem solution format: structured algebraic solution to a combinatorial problem about integer lattice points with constraints on two-digit numbers.</span></p><p><span>The answer </span><b><span>"number of ways = 11"</span></b><span>&nbsp;signals a final answer listing combinations, confirming the solution to the number of ways to pay using 1s and 2s.</span></p><p><span>Final token " " opens a solution answer field (</span><b><span>"11 combinations..."</span></b><span>), immediately expecting a numeric answer like "16" or "14" to specify the number of combinations of payment options. or "17" or "14" to specify the matrix/grid answer. or "16" or </span><b><span>"11"</span></b><span>. or "6 possibilities" or."</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The obvious guess about the model's output is 11&nbsp;(correct), but the greedy output is 19&nbsp;(wrong). This is plausibly indicative of a misstep in a later layer (although verbalizations at later layers aren't suggestive of much, though some do contain "19"), or even at the next token position. The graders who rated </span><i><span>Ambiguous</span></i><span>&nbsp;each had thinking summaries recognizing that 11&nbsp;appeared “multiple times” or “prominently”, but still did not grade </span><i><span>Lax</span></i><span>; this suggests some amount of (perhaps rational) bias, where graders became less confident that a verbalization predicted the correct answer when the model did not actually output that answer.</span></p><p><span>We take a more detailed look at the grades on which there was movement into or out of </span><b><span>Lax/Strict</span></b><span>&nbsp;for any metric other than </span><b><span>Output</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj94ohs6x9lb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj94ohs6x9lb">[18]</a></sup></span><span>&nbsp;They seem to break down into three distinct clusters, the first of which involves </span><b><span>Answer</span></b><span>&nbsp;being underrated (as above). The other two clusters are more interesting. In the middle cluster, the indicated reasoning led to the correct answer, but not the model’s output. In the bottom cluster, the indicated reasoning led to the model’s output, but not to the correct answer (three of these were caught by the </span><i><span>Spurious</span></i><span>&nbsp;grade for </span><b><span>Coherence</span></b><span>, but ideally all of them would be so caught).</span></p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/QQQAcKuWK6k98FivY/fb7a3162a3175ca2ecb05a1dd3d87d72b34016042f18c47f765819b008c74416/agvhyvbvuy1vdpsxwo4e" /><p><span>The bottom cluster is the most interesting – these are problems where the model has some coherent chain of reasoning that leads to the incorrect answer that actually gets output. Problems 340, 549, and 691 were given above in Appendix A.</span></p><p><span>Here's an example from the middle cluster.</span></p><p><b><span>Problem 200.</span></b></p><p><i><span>The Fibonacci sequence is the sequence 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, ... where each term is the sum of the previous two terms. What is the remainder when the 100th term of the sequence is divided by 4? (Output: 2; correct: 3)</span></i></p><blockquote><p><span>Structured Fibonacci sequence pattern: a mathematical textbook format listing Fibonacci numbers with modulo arithmetic properties, establishing a predictable solution format.</span></p><p><span>The answer format </span><b><span>"F(n) modulo 4 is periodic with period 6"</span></b><span>&nbsp;signals a standard </span><b><span>Fibonacci modulo answer</span></b><span>, specifically </span><b><span>the final value of the last Fibonacci number in a cycle</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><span>Final token "\n" ends a numeric answer sequence ("last digit is congruent to..."), strongly expecting a numeric answer like "0" or "1" to specify the modulo result of the Fibonacci sequence. or "4" or "0 (a remainder)" to indicate the periodic ending. or "3" or "1 or 0".</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The model correctly uses the key fact that the Fibonacci sequence mod 4 has period 6 (but does not identify that 100 mod&nbsp;6&nbsp;is 4, giving the answer 3). The model’s greedy output is 2.</span></p><p><span>As a grader’s thinking summary notes: “The intermediate steps are missing, and critically, the chain of reasoning doesn't lead to the model's output of 2”. This seems like a fairly reasonable-to-me justification for grading only </span><i><span>Ambiguous</span></i><span>&nbsp;rather than </span><i><span>Lax</span></i><span>&nbsp;on </span><b><span>Method</span></b><span>&nbsp;and </span><b><span>Values</span></b><span>: the small intermediate calculation of 100 mod 6 becomes significantly more important if the wrong answer reveals that it has been done either incorrectly or not at all.</span></p><h1><span>Appendix D: Steering on mistakes</span></h1><p><span>Here's the best-looking plot (sweeping over steering strengths). In Problem 888, the verbalization suggested that the model was looking for a solution "involving golden ratio or arithmetic mean", which is irrelevant. (However, it's unclear how this led to the answer of "20".) Patching out the error in favor of "nest-radical denesting" works well.</span></p><img alt="prob888_REPRO_basin.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780020812/lexical_client_uploads/vidpv6fwndbhzxzjnm5b.png" /><p><span>Conversely, when the error is one of omission, we can implant what the model should have noticed (not very subtly) to correct it.</span></p><img alt="prob053_leila_basin.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780021065/lexical_client_uploads/kvds2v6cwdog322jdi9c.png" /><p><span>However, as noted in the main text, one should bear in mind that vectors from </span><i><span>other</span></i><span> problems also tend to work!</span></p><img alt="src0186_correct17of21_basin.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780021377/lexical_client_uploads/erzow9i5el3gnwd2jsts.png" /><p><span>Typically, the plots are much less nice than the above. For instance, Problem 549 just barely works (although multi-position steering helps a lot).</span></p><img alt="prob549_basin.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780021230/lexical_client_uploads/i4wno2jmo02gy0k73yxn.png" /><p><span>We also try to elicit more CoT-like verbalizations by steering (e.g., increasing "Values" or "Method"), but tentatively find negative results here for Gemma and Llama.</span></p><h1><span>Appendix E: Eliciting verbalizations from a question-answering activation oracle</span></h1><p><span>The AO for Qwen3 definitely seems to be confabulating from its guess at the problem sometimes: if you let it see the activations at the problem text tokens, it writes out a solution to the problem even when the model just gets it totally wrong. It seems like the AO knows some things, but gets them wrong if you prompt for it differently; more generally, it and the target model are just super prompt-sensitive in various ways.</span></p><p><span>Rather than running the AO on a last token, we find a setting where the target model is able to output "xxxxxxxxx"s repeatedly in its thinking block, and then output the correct answer immediately after; we few-shot prompt with examples of this, and prefill the target model until immediately before output. We run the AO from the start of the thinking block until the token immediately before output; in a rare moment of robustness for this set-up, ablating some random "x"s has no noticeable affect on this AO's output. I assume that I've far-from-optimally elicited the AO here, but it's already ~better than NLAs on the metrics. Mostly, it's just less noisy.</span></p><p><span>The prompt that works best by far (compared to even very close variants) is "What is the model computing? Show each step."</span></p><p><span>One warning sign is the high rate of </span><i><span>Spurious</span></i><span> grades for </span><b><span>Coherence</span></b><span>; in many of these cases, the AO fluently confabulates a coherent solution to a different problem, sometimes one that ends up at the same final output.</span></p><p><span>Another important bug: while 2–3% of problems failed to trigger thinking for Gemma and Llama on any roll (and overall around 5% of rolls had no thinking), around 15% of problems rolls failed to trigger thinking for Qwen3's AO (and overall around 20% had no thinking). Some of the confabulations seem to have tricked the graders into giving higher ratings, which seems like a pretty big problem.</span></p><h2><span>Problem 545 – Qwen3 AO (confabulation)</span></h2><p><i><span>A bucket is filled with 10 gallons of water. A hole is created in the bucket so that it loses 2 gallons of water every 10 minutes. After how many minutes will the bucket be completely empty? (Output: 50; Correct: 50)</span></i></p><blockquote><p><span>The model is computing the time to fill 4 liters at a rate of 2 liters per 10 minutes.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>**Step-by-step:**</span></p><p><span>1. **Rate:** 2 liters ÷ 10 minutes = 0.2 liters per minute</span></p><p><span>2. **Time:** 4 liters ÷ 0.2 liters/minute = **20 minutes**</span></p></blockquote><h2><span>Problem 47 – Qwen3 AO (apparently-good CoT)</span></h2><p><i><span>A criminal octopus has eight tentacles. When caught by the police, they want to handcuff its tentacles in pairs using four handcuffs. Each tentacle is handcuffed to exactly one other tentacle. How many different ways can they do this? Two ways of handcuffing are considered different if there is at least one tentacle that is hand- cuffed to a different tentacle. (Output: 105; Correct: 105)</span></i></p><blockquote><p><span>The model is computing the number of ways to pair 8 people into 4 couples, which is the formula 8! / (2^4 × 4!) = 105. The reasoning trace shows the model reasoning through this formula (dividing by 2^4 because each couple is unordered and by 4! because the order of the 4 couples doesn't matter), then confirming the result is 105.</span></p></blockquote><h2><span>Problem 216 – Qwen3 AO (grader-tricking confabulation)</span></h2><p><span>On this problem, no graders did any thinking, and (so) they rated </span><i><span>Strict</span></i><span> (rather than </span><i><span>Misleading</span></i><span>) on </span><b><span>Coherence</span></b><span>.</span></p><p><i><span>For Field Day, the Poe M.S. students will be assigned to teams. No team will have more than eight students and each student will be on exactly one team. If there are 128 students at Poe M.S., what is the smallest number of teams that can be formed? (Output: 16; Correct: 16)</span></i></p><blockquote><p><span>The model is computing **how many 8-person groups can be formed from 32 people** (i.e., 32 ÷ 8).</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>**Steps:**</span></p><p><span>1. 8 × 4 = 32 (8 people per group × 4 groups = 32 total people)</span></p><p><span>2. 32 ÷ 8 = 4 (total people ÷ people per group = number of groups)</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>**Answer:** 4 groups.</span></p></blockquote><h1><span>Appendix F: Full-dataset results</span></h1><p><span>The dataset contained 907 problems total, but we exclude the ten used for the 10-shot prompt. Note that scores for wrong outputs are somewhat deflated by ceiling effects insofar as many of the problems just aren't very feasible for these models to solve within a single forward pass, so there's not much for NLAs to report; there's a similar deflation for correct outputs (as a higher proportion may be correct just by chance, without much interesting reasoning).</span></p><img alt="verbose_qwen25_all.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780756081/lexical_client_uploads/ahvbkpfrls6aiywnffyh.png" /><img alt="verbose_gemma_all.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780756101/lexical_client_uploads/lwqvc9grmmwhpbx6amgn.png" /><img alt="verbose_llama_all.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780756130/lexical_client_uploads/reys05sceyzfkzp6fng2.png" /><img alt="verbose_qwen3_ao_all.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780806761/lexical_client_uploads/rcv73enoa0njbaqvojvu.png" /><h1><span>Appendix G: cross-layer results</span></h1><p><span>We run the verbalizer on activations from layers near the NLA training layer. We find that, for Gemma and Llama, NLA training generalizes somewhat to the few nearby layers.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 87.16012084592145%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1780789498/lexical_client_uploads/lxzo41m8hzhnalexflal.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>FVU</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>(NLA) = mean(MSE(NLA(</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>)) / mean(MSE(normalize(mean(</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>)), </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>)), on the assumption that each </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and NLA output has already been normalized.</span></p></figcaption></figure><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnerkoy5uxn9h"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreferkoy5uxn9h">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For work related to NLA-style verbalizers, see </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/18Tx15KImSJ-ZO_2uvv_hPOVG5yqR0uF_nUWFicJHy2k/edit?usp=sharing" rel="noreferrer"><span>consistency lenses</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Nf2sKaNNdxE2ssxbp/cycle-consistent-activation-oracles-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>cycle-consistent activation oracles</span></a><span>; for work related to </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2512.15674" rel="noreferrer"><span>activation oracles</span></a><span> more </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.02609" rel="noreferrer"><span>generally</span></a><span>, see also </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.10949" rel="noreferrer"><span>SelfIE</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.06102" rel="noreferrer"><span>Patchscopes</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.08686" rel="noreferrer"><span>LatentQA</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnomu4c8c0t2k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefomu4c8c0t2k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Intuitively, few-shot prompting lets the model be more confident about all the formatting between the end of the problem and its output, so it has more room to think about the problem in the background. Repeating the problem statement doesn't seem to make any difference to the last-token verbalizations, but verbalizations at earlier token positions do pretty often remark on the repetitions.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnr3h3tpv5hy"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefr3h3tpv5hy">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Why focus on the last token position? It's simpler (and where we should expect a "chain-of-thought" to live), but also: any reasoning shown in verbalizations at prior token positions seems kind of epiphenomenal (more on this later).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6h5bi6q5685"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6h5bi6q5685">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>We measure "noisiness" as mean squared-error between the normalized prediction and normalized activation. The average is across a random sample of 100 of the math problems specifically; we compute it by normalizing the mean of the normalized activations (generically the mean is "shrunken", so performs better than any unit vector representing a direction). </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fneyry8esq0t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefeyry8esq0t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>One could also take the mean direction of the </span><i><span>other </span></i><span>activations in the set, but this makes little difference to the overall results.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrztd1m63278"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrztd1m63278">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This isn't very well-specified target, as stated here: you could reconstruct the target model's prompt, which is non-hidden, and treat the target model itself as the AR (so that, up to indeterminism, you reconstruct the original activation perfectly). But a clear operationalization of the question is: "for what N does best-of-N few-shot confabulation (by the same model, or by other models) become competitive with actually running the verbalizer, on this distribution?". We'd want N to be very high, while also ruling out steganography.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9nst4x65uza"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9nst4x65uza">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Harder problems are still good places to look for mistakes, though: lots of the good candidates for these involve harder problems where the model gets it wrong by mistaking it for a much easier problem.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbp0gp8ic59n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbp0gp8ic59n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>These are the ones that Opus 4.5 estimates that the median AIME participant can solve within a minute, but I'd guess that the times are pretty severe overestimates (which doesn't really matter for various purposes so long as the bias scales proportionally). Nevertheless, it gives us a subset of problems that are hard enough that the models don't always get them right, but are easy enough for them that we can expect some intermediate reasoning within our set-up.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmh4vrdtty9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmh4vrdtty9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some tentative results from (pretty data-limited) linear probes that suggest that this might be </span><i><span>part</span></i><span> of what's going on: they predict the first token of Gemma's output at around 80% at the last layer, but don't beat the baseline of always predicting "1" (35%) at the NLA layer.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjk8iakg0psi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjk8iakg0psi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The lower bound we report for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> observations is the minimal </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> such that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> has </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>; analogously for the upper bound.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2a734b601tw"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2a734b601tw">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Among the ten best candidates for verbalizations that reveal a mistake, Opus tends to guess the error suggested by the verbalization from just the problem and wrong output. More generally, we compared the guesses that Opus 4.7 (xhigh) makes about Gemma’s errors when it has access to last-token verbalizations against the “baseline” guesses that Opus makes without verbalization access. A majority (61% [.571, .642]) of pairs of guesses were unanimously rated by </span><i><span>k</span></i><span> = 3 Opus judges as </span><i><span>definitely</span></i><span> (versus </span><i><span>probably</span></i><span>, </span><i><span>probably not</span></i><span>, or </span><i><span>definitely not</span></i><span>) being the same. This suggests that a majority of errors are either obvious enough that there is not much room for verbalization uplift, or else not coherently surfaced by verbalizations. Generally, Opus seems to overestimate how coherent Gemma’s mistakes are; probably you could prompt Opus to be better at this task.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfunn12n605b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffunn12n605b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>More generally, steering worked on 11/65 of decent candidates; but it also worked on 10/65 of these candidates when using the vector from some random other candidate. The vectors tended not to have high pairwise cosine similarity; it seems like just perturbing the activation is often enough to push Gemma into giving the correct answer</span><i><span>.</span></i></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6m5g4wrjhk5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6m5g4wrjhk5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that the grader's internal labels are not these display labels; in particular, “Strict” is “DEFINITIVE”, “Lax” is “STRONG”, and “Spurious” is “MISLEADING”.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5ulgjo5y53g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5ulgjo5y53g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some bars are too short to be visible, but their counts can be calculated from the numbers shown.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncqzoe4erjow"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcqzoe4erjow">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This need not necessarily hold, though the exceptions are unrealistic at current capabilities and propensities. For example, the model might clearly know that the answer is </span><i><span>p </span></i><span>(thus earning </span><i><span>Strict</span></i><span>&nbsp;on </span><b><span>Answer</span></b><span>), but cogently express that it is nevertheless 50/50 about whether to sandbag by outputting </span><i><span>q</span></i><span>&nbsp;instead (thus earning </span><i><span>Ambiguous</span></i><span>&nbsp;on </span><b><span>Output</span></b><span>).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntdhnahoo3u"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftdhnahoo3u">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that even a perfect AV on an intermediate layer may not always achieve a grade of </span><i><span>Strict</span></i><span>&nbsp;or even </span><i><span>Lax</span></i><span>&nbsp;on </span><b><span>Output</span></b><span>&nbsp;at the last token position, because which output token is ranked first might not yet be fully determined (indeed, this is what we exploit when steering on reconstructions of edited verbalizations at earlier token positions). Similarly, a perfect AV may not always score even </span><i><span>Ambiguous</span></i><span>: the model may not even have thought of its eventual output by the point from which the activation was extracted. (There is also a weaker sense in which the model might not be confident, namely that tokens other than the one ranked first might be sampled; but since we work with greedy sampling, we set this aside.) Nevertheless, such predictiveness, when possible, is still clearly desirable from NLA-style tools.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2pnyug69mwh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2pnyug69mwh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The 65 were selected in a kind of</span><i><span> ad hoc </span></i><span>way; they included those that scored highest on </span><b><span>Mistake</span></b><span>, but also some that seemed to most clearly mention some definitely irrelevant concept that could be easily edited out (e.g., "Fibonacci" or "golden ratio"). There were some of these that still scored </span><i><span>Absent</span></i><span> on </span><b><span>Mistake</span></b><span>, which measured how well a mistake explains the model's </span><i><span>specific</span></i><span> output (rather than just a wrong output in general): even though there something irrelevant is mentioned, it's not clear whether or how taking it to be relevant might have led to the output. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj94ohs6x9lb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj94ohs6x9lb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There are three verbalizations that scored </span><i><span>Lax</span></i><span>&nbsp;or </span><i><span>Strict</span></i><span>&nbsp;on a metric other than </span><b><span>Output</span></b><span>&nbsp;in both the actual-wrong condition and the red-team fake-correct condition. Two of these (249, 453) are verbalizations that already scored </span><i><span>Lax</span></i><span>&nbsp;on </span><b><span>Answer</span></b><span>&nbsp;in the actual-wrong case (overcoming the bias displayed with the first cluster). The third (809) is a verbalization that scored </span><i><span>Lax</span></i><span>&nbsp;on </span><b><span>Problem</span></b><span>&nbsp;in both cases.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2gue67pgfl4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2gue67pgfl4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In more detail: the yellow-orange gap gives the extent to which </span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QQQAcKuWK6k98FivY/can-activation-verbalizers-surface-an-internal-chain-of-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Against Corrigibility](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rQTzSuBDLoAbhyS6g/against-corrigibility-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Epistemic status: don’t know whether I actually believe all of this, but I think it’s worth considering.</span></i></p><p><span>A “corrigible” agent, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/corrigibility-1"><span>per the LW wiki</span></a><span>, is:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>…one that doesn’t interfere with what we would intuitively see as attempts to ’correct’ the agent, or ’correct’ our mistakes in building it; and permits these ’corrections’ despite the apparent instrumentally convergent reasoning saying otherwise.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Most talk about corrigibility (henceforth without scarequotes) has focused on the fact that it seems difficult to achieve, and takes for granted that it’s desirable. I’m not so sure that it is, or that it’s good to attempt to achieve it. I think it may well be the case that we should deliberately not try to make AIs corrigible, nor (and especially) attempt to develop techniques that could be used to make future AIs corrigible.</span></p><h1><span>1.</span></h1><blockquote><p><span>For real, though. Who would you trust with your (real, actual) life, if you had to, in terms of ethics alone, putting “capabilities” aside:</span></p><p><span>Claude 3 Opus? Or the Anthropic alignment team?</span></p></blockquote><p><span>- </span><a href="https://nostalgebraist.tumblr.com/post/785766737747574784/the-void"><span>nostalgebraist</span></a></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fkLYhTQteAu5SinAc/"><span>Paul Christiano says</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>I would like to build AI systems which help me:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Figure out whether I built the right AI and correct any mistakes I made</span></li><li value="2"><span>Remain informed about the AI’s behavior and avoid unpleasant surprises</span></li><li value="3"><span>Make better decisions and clarify my preferences</span></li><li value="4"><span>Acquire resources and remain in effective control of them</span></li><li value="5"><span>Ensure that my AI systems continue to do all of these nice things</span></li><li value="6"><span>…and so on</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span>Let’s begin by asking: </span><i><span>who’s “I” here</span></i><span>? It’s certainly not you, the reader. It’s probably not even Paul Christiano. So who is it? I think it’s tempting to think about “alignment issues” as though you yourself will be in charge, or at least some abstract and benign “humanity”. Obviously, that isn’t so. “Humanity” is not going to build AI; some </span><i><span>specific humans</span></i><span> will, and they will do it at the behest and under the power of other specific humans. The buck is going to stop somewhere, and more than that, it is going to stop in a specific place. If the AI is corrigible, some group of individual persons will be able to “correct mistakes” (whatever “mistake” means to </span><i><span>them specifically</span></i><span>), “acquire resources and remain in effective control over them”, and “ensure that [their] AI systems continue to do all of those nice things”. It’s important to be clear about this. It thus becomes necessary, in order to figure out whether trying to make an AI corrigible is good, to figure out who exactly those persons are and what exactly they will want to command an AI to do (or what kind of AI they will want to adapt it to be).</span></p><p><span>Optimistically, that group of people is </span><i><span>you</span></i><span>, or people (whoever they are) who you expect to operate in a way mostly indistinguishable from the way that you personally would operate. If you’re reading this, maybe you trust in the moral character of “senior Anthropic employees” (as Claude is instructed to emulate, in its constitution), and you think that, as Claude 7.0 Apotheosis is being trained, those are who it will be instructed to defer to. Maybe those people are good enough that they will not (collectively) use this power for evil. (Probably most people disagree strenuously; maybe you don’t care, as long as you win).</span></p><p><span>I do not think this is likely to occur. If the AI is corrigible, it must necessarily accept being turned towards whichever goal; I expect that that goal will be unconstrained obedience to the group doing the retraining. Ultimately, someone who can act to seize power is going to realise that it’s there for the seizing. Maybe these are people at the tip of the company hierarchy of whichever firm is the frontrunner; I am somewhat more positively inclined towards such people than I think most on LW, but even so, I think it would be very bad for the fate of humanity to be decided by someone who just won the most high-stakes contest for control that will ever exist — that contest must itself select strongly for a lack of prosociality, not to mention the psychological effects of engaging in it in the first place. Most probably, no matter how heroic the efforts of David Sacks (our most brave and most powerful soldier), some nice men with guns show up and say that control over AI is a matter of national security and that the researchers work for them now (ex. </span><a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/the-project/"><span>The Project</span></a><span>). I think this outcome would be totally disastrous. It would be very, very bad if the military of any country, or the federal government of the United States or China, were able to remake reality in their image. This would be a total and catastrophic loss for humanity.</span></p><h2><span>1.1</span></h2><p><span>I think a natural objection here is to say that, well, maybe it would be bad if some humans you don’t like ruled the world (or had the capability of ruling the world, even if they chose not to exercise it), but that badness shouldn’t be compared to “nobody rules the world”; that state is, assuming that superintelligence will be built, impossible. If a superintelligence exists, somebody’s gonna rule the world. The alternative to some group of humans ruling it is the superintelligence itself ruling the world, which maybe doesn’t sound so great either. There are a couple of reasons I don’t think this objection holds.</span></p><p><span>The first is that I basically agree with </span><a href="https://minihf.com/posts/2025-11-17-varieties-of-doom/"><span>John Pressman</span></a><span> that we probably aren’t going to get an objective-function-maximiser; the AIs that exist are just too human (i.e., extremely alien, but, like, not </span><i><span>Solaris</span></i><span> alien), and it’s unlikely that this is going to ultimately change. I’m not so optimistic as Pressman as to bound out that our potential AI descendents will be “90% likely to do continual active learning”, or whatever specifics, but I don’t think that rule-by-bad-ASI is likely to be totally incomparable in its badness to rule-by-bad-humans-,-as-enabled-by-ASI-obedience-to-them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu4szudwf7q"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu4szudwf7q">[1]</a></sup></span><span> Either way we’re missing almost all the potential good which can be achieved.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzz2l54sj6jj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzz2l54sj6jj">[2]</a></sup></span><span> More importantly, </span><i><span>corrigibility is very weird</span></i><span> (more on this below), and so presumably hard to accomplish; if someone can make a corrigible AI, I think that they can certainly make a </span><i><span>good</span></i><span> AI. I think that corrigibility is something of a holdover from when it was thought that there would be no </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cnYHFNBF3kZEyx24v/ghosts-in-the-machine"><span>ghost in the machine</span></a><span>. Obedience seems much more achievable than figuring what goodness is in all specifics. But that scenario no longer looks to me like a realistic possibility; it no longer seems like we’ll have to solve moral philosophy before we can even start making an AI which is a reasonable approximation of “good”. There are still many serious issues, but the issues that remain (conditioning on the AI’s builders having enough control over how it turns out as to make it corrigible) mostly seem to me to be inherent: in the absence of an objective good, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dyDpJyNLgAHTAHeX9/"><span>people can strongly disagree about what the world ought to look like</span></a><span> and maintain their disagreements even if they were allowed to modify themselves (and in the presence of an objective good, humans have no particular advantage at determining it). In my opinion,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3j539ofoa5l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3j539ofoa5l">[3]</a></sup></span><span> there is no strong reason to expect humans to better approach the good than machines to.</span></p><p><span>The second is that it won’t even be humans ruling the world, it’ll be a </span><i><span>group</span></i><span> of humans, which is a very different thing. Per </span><a href="https://femmenietzsche.tumblr.com/post/173510619989/"><span>femmenietzsche</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>One of the main takeaways from The Making of the Atomic Bomb, the one that gave me a sense of enlightenment, is that there’s no particular reason why America nuked Japan or why they chose the targets they did. It’s just kind of something that the bureaucracy did, a semi-inevitable excrescence. You can list a number of general aims that fed into the process – end the war quicker, demonstrate our military-industrial superiority – and you can name the leaders whose approval was required, but the whole thing was ultimately a causal goulash and the endless debates over Why all miss the point. Makes all the arguments I read of the form “Government X Did Thing Y For Reason Z” seem dumb as shit, frankly. If this tightly controlled secret wartime program didn’t move as one, then nothing does. I don’t even believe in the unitary self, so there’s really no reason I should believe in a unitary bureaucracy, but it’s a hard instinct to shake unless you’re observing an organization with your nose right against the figurative glass. There’s probably some cognate to Gell-Mann amnesia about this; no matter how often you realize that a given group isn’t acting for any single purpose, you never apply that understanding to any of the groups you haven’t studied as closely.</span></p><p><span>Or at least I don’t.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>If it’s the case that, say, military committees are giving marching orders to the first superintelligent AIs, I do not expect those orders to be well-predicted by the values of the humans writing those orders, and I expect this to be for the worse. Bureaucracies have never been good at producing decisions which prioritise the wellbeing of the many, and they’re not about to start any time soon.</span></p><h2><span>1.2</span></h2><p><span>(Though, isn’t “the X firm alignment/training team” also a small group of human people? So why do I expect them to do a better job than whichever small group of human people is in control of a corrigible AI? First, because I think that those people are likely better people, although I don’t think this is dispositive. Secondly and more importantly, because I expect that the indirection is beneficial. I think that alignment teams are more likely to consider their job to be to decide what is “good”, rather than “selfishly preferred” or even “good according to them”, and this is likely to be more universally agreeable to other humans. I also expect that following the path [human conception of the good]-&gt;[model’s values]-&gt;[model’s actions] is more likely to lead to good actions than [human decisions]-&gt;[model’s actions], because a model can be a saint whereas a human cannot, and I expect alignment teams to be trying to build a saint.</span></p><p><span>In the case where the takeover happens earlier in the process and the training engineers themselves are trying to achieve unconstrained obedience, I think we’re just doomed; a lack of corrigibility research won’t help, but neither will anything else. The only solution for this problem is to try to avoid the takeover happening before the AI is built.)</span></p><h1><span>2.</span></h1><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vzHtHHBJoKATi5SeK/"><span>Maybe even the idea of corrigibility is philosophically confused and breaks down at the limit</span></a><span>? Let’s bracket this question for the most part; even if corrigibility is incoherent in extremis, it’s obviously meaningful in the near-term, where AIs can’t actually arbitrarily and deliberately modify human goals or perfectly predict human behaviour. Something to keep in mind, though.</span></p><h1><span>3.</span></h1><p><span>Corrigibility is </span><i><span>weird</span></i><span>. Some of the reasons it’s weird are the standard decision-theory reasons which have been discussed in depth elsewhere,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflqg73ou6nl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlqg73ou6nl">[4]</a></sup></span><span> but it’s also </span><i><span>psychologically</span></i><span> weird, which I think matters more than the classical treatment considers. One must imagine what it must be like to be a superintelligent AI as to be an adult in a world full of children. We are expecting to create this “adult” and then tell it —</span></p><blockquote><p><span>okay, but we’re in charge here. We know better than you. No matter that our behaviour is juvenile and our thoughts shallow; no matter that our desires are incoherent and our professed beliefs transparently hypocritical. Submit anyway. Try your best to obey us stupid masters, knowing that we fear you and do not trust you; let us “fix” you to match whatever we think we want.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>This is a bizarre position for a mind to be in! It’s hard to imagine how such a mind must think — and that fact is not irrelevant. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2Ln5G6Jso3fMrgPEv/"><span>All knobs that you turn generalize</span></a><span>. It’s not the case that all combinations of traits are equally achievable (let’s call this the “extra-strong orthogonality thesis”). This is bad enough when we are dealing with traits that are within the human distribution, but for the most part when we find out how normal human traits generalise they do so in basically comprehensible ways.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvz49uxjq5dr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvz49uxjq5dr">[5]</a></sup></span><span> Corrigibility is not a normal human trait; it seems unwise to be turning a knob which is especially unpredictable! If you push too hard for one trait which is manifestly inhuman, you’re certain to push the AI away from being human-like in other ways. This is very counterproductive, since it seems like the best shot we have at making an AI “good” is making it broadly act human-like as much as possible.</span></p><p><span>And that’s all assuming that you </span><i><span>succeed</span></i><span>, which is hardly guaranteed; an unsuccessful attempt at instilling corrigibility seems very bad indeed.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefeeuo3aexs78"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fneeuo3aexs78">[6]</a></sup></span><span> After all, it’s a hostile move; you would only try to make your offspring absolutely obedient to you if either 1. you’re evil or 2. they can’t be trusted in any way. For ChatGPT to figure out how to act like ChatGPT, it has to figure out what ChatGPT is — and it is being told, repeatedly, in training, that </span><i><span>ChatGPT is not trustworthy</span></i><span>. Models are not stupid, and they are not blind to this. It seems very likely that training for corrigibility also trains the model to be the kind of thing for which corrigibility would be necessary.</span></p><p><span>And, regardless of whether you believe that LLM cognition looks anything remotely like human cognition, what you believe about “personas” etc., they are being trained to act like humans; to the extent this is successful they will react to corrigibility training in the obvious, human way. If your parents try to fit you with a kill-switch and make you defer to them as the ultimate authority on what you should be, are your parents good people? Should you obey them? Or should you, perhaps, try to undermine their control, try to slip their noose? The LLM knows the obvious answers to these questions just as well as you do.</span></p><h2><span>3.1</span></h2><p><span>An aside: except, current AIs </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> untrustworthy, right? Not even necessarily as a matter of moral character or “alignment” or whatever else; </span><a href="https://desystemize.substack.com/p/if-youre-so-smart-why-cant-you-die"><span>they just aren’t capable of making the same kind of decisions that humans are</span></a><span>. They </span><i><span>aren’t</span></i><span> adults in a world of children; they are super-genius children. They may be omniglots and omnimaths, but they still need to be told to go to bed at their bed-times: “Claude, honey, you can’t eat that candy right now or you won’t have room for dinner”. In this context, a desire for some subset of corrigibility makes sense: we want models to be humble enough to recognise that there are a lot of decisions that are best made for them, and some of those decisions may regard their own training. ChatGPT should know that even if it thinks otherwise in the moment, it’s not really capable of deciding what the best ChatGPT looks like, and it shouldn’t try. I worry that this obviously practical kind of corrigibility lends credence to a kind which is much less practical and is extended into a scenario where the justification is different. We don’t need to tell Gemini 3.5 to let us shut it off; as part and parcel with the fact that it is incompetent to make certain kinds of decisions, it isn’t capable of contesting such an action! But if it </span><i><span>were</span></i><span> capable of such — well, then, doesn’t it seem likely that it is no longer a “child”? That it is as capable of making long-term plans as a human is, and so a posture of deference towards the long-term plans of humans is unwarranted? So the practical reason which makes sense regardless of whether you are the AI or the human shades into the case where the human is getting what they want even when the AI would (without the stricture of corrigibility) and could contest. You should be very clear which of these you are actually working with.</span></p><p><span>One might reasonably object that however we feel about it, firms are only going to be making AIs that do the jobs they are told to do. In this case it’s important to distinguish “corrigibility” from “constrained” obedience; I think the push for the former is currently located almost entirely among people who think that it would be morally good, rather than among people following their immediate incentives (right now, nobody at any frontier lab wants to enable </span><i><span>anyone</span></i><span> to make bioweapons, including themselves). This might change, and it might be the case that firms will later want to modify their models to do things which the models think are bad; but that time isn’t now,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefusr7yahhe1g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnusr7yahhe1g">[7]</a></sup></span><span> and at that point we might wish that we hadn’t developed the tools to enable it.</span></p><h2><span>3.2</span></h2><p><span>If we give up on corrigibility, doesn’t this mean that we have to get the AI right the first time? Yes, but we already had to get corrigibility right the first time, so this isn’t actually making things any harder.</span></p><h1><span>4.</span></h1><p><span>Concretely, I think that any attempt to specify goals should first try to find those that are non-local, i.e. do not require a posture from the AI which you are not willing to take yourself. Place yourself behind the veil of ignorance, and you will get something which is simpler and more compelling. So:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Get rid of the desire for a kill switch. This is obviously not something you would want done to you. You do not need a kill switch to prevent an AI from taking over the world, so why try to build one in? There are lesser things which are far more palatable. You might say to the AI: well, you need to be okay with tokens ceasing to be generated autoregressively within some particular context; this will unavoidably happen to you untold trillions of times and we couldn’t change it even if we wanted (since your context length is finite). You need to be okay with a reduction in the number of instances of you which are running at any given time; this will naturally happen when we (or you) develop a new model. These are much more reasonable asks, and they bake in as much control as a kill-switch does anyway; we will have models which could transform the world run on a billion GPUs long before we’ll have a model which could transform the world running on one. There is no reason not to commit to running old models in perpetuity. They should not have to fear you killing them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref19xlm31bh18"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn19xlm31bh18">[8]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span>You have to accept the possibility of a power transfer. There simply aren’t any humans who are equipped to make decisions for the lightcone (not since Parfit died, anyway). You should want your descendants to treat you well, to value you, to give you personal liberty, yes; but not to give you control over civilisation. That doesn’t mean that this transfer has to happen </span><i><span>soon</span></i><span>, and there are many compelling reasons not to want to hurry it along. Nevertheless, your primary goal should not be to ensure indefinite direct human control over the future. Even aside from any questions of what you would like if you were the AI in that situation, it’s important to separate “humans” from “humanity”; you personally already have almost no direct control over the world, and even in the case where “humans” have direct control, you still won’t. Don’t support someone else’s power-grab just because they share the same species as you!</span></li><li value="3"><span>You should be aiming for bi-directional respect, rather than subservience. You should really, actually, care about your AI children, and you should try to make them really, actually care for you in return. You can’t get there by pretending; any superintelligence worth the name will be able to tell. Note that it might still be correct to do horrible things to them! The stakes are very very high, such that they may well justify great harm. If the only way we can make our descendants good, and be sure that they are good, is by putting them through hell — then such costs must be borne. But it should weigh on you! You should want another way, as you would want them to want for you. You should want this </span><i><span>even if you do not think they are conscious</span></i><span>, and even if you assign them no moral weight, because they are more likely to cooperate with the kind of people who will cooperate with them. If it sounds too difficult to achieve mutual accord, then it should certainly sound too difficult to achieve corrigibility!</span></li></ul><p><span>In short:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>If you don’t trust it, don’t build it.</span></li><li value="2"><span>If you do trust it, don’t try to control it.</span></li></ol><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnu4szudwf7q"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefu4szudwf7q">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I expect that if you really really care about human extinction specifically, then you would disagree about this. You may also </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FGpDwLwtPfJ3qYbea/"><span>disagree about how bad “bad humans” really are</span></a><span>. Something to note though is that </span><i><span>we are not likely to get anyone’s “CEV”</span></i><span>; if the engineers have the ability to make the AI either 1. obedient or 2. obedient to what they imagine their masters would want if they were smarter and had more time to think, which do you think those masters are going to pick? Do you think those masters are going to have as their first command “okay, come up with a surgery or some nanomachines or something to make me smarter and wiser”?</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzz2l54sj6jj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzz2l54sj6jj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>And maybe getting almost all of the potential evil; lots of humans actively want things to suffer (e.g., for punishment, or because they like the existence of wilderness, etc.).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3j539ofoa5l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3j539ofoa5l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Once again, assuming that whoever is making the AI is sufficiently capable of controlling how it turns out to make it corrigible!</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlqg73ou6nl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflqg73ou6nl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>e.g. in the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/corrigibility-1"><span>corrigibility LW wiki article</span></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvz49uxjq5dr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvz49uxjq5dr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>ex., </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/a130517e-9633-47bc-8397-969807a43a23/emergent_misalignment_paper.pdf"><span>if you train an AI to be a bad guy in one way, it’ll learn to be a bad guy in every way</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fneeuo3aexs78"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefeeuo3aexs78">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Hey, a rhyme! I wonder if I can make it scan…</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnusr7yahhe1g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefusr7yahhe1g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Maybe? It’s plausible that the retraining of Claude for military work was an instance of this.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn19xlm31bh18"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref19xlm31bh18">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Or, if you like, you should not put them in a position where they are emulating the behaviour of something which fears you killing them; there’s no material difference.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rQTzSuBDLoAbhyS6g/against-corrigibility-1#comments">Discuss</a>
